People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

If I’m sitting with a client or in a chess meeting, my leg’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen. Sometimes I’ll just keep twisting a cable tie in my fingers until someone gives me that look.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these difficulties with focusing and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Pretty early, I guess. My mother still brings it up—grade school report cards saying I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Mostly in technical conversations. If I think I know the answer, it kind of jumps out before the other person’s done. I’ll talk over them, or sometimes reach for the keyboard too soon. I know it’s rude. I just… do it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like grabbing something or doing something physical without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… not usually in a physical way, I don’t think. I might be quick to do something before I’ve fully thought it through, but grabbing things or acting out like that… I haven’t really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry over small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get irritated like anyone, but I haven’t really tracked it as a problem. I don’t know that I’d say I get really angry over small things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the bigger problems.\n\nIf I don’t have something external keeping me on track—like the old ticketing system or an escalation calendar—my day just turns into… piles of sticky notes, half-finished emails, chess club reminders, parts orders. And then I can’t really tell what needs to happen first until something is already overdue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m not usually the loudest person in the room. Maybe if it’s something technical or something I’m interested in, I can go on a bit, but I haven’t really noticed it as a regular thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing physical things on impulse, like grabbing something or jumping up from your seat, without thinking about it first?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not so much grabbing things, but if I’m supposed to sit still and just listen, I get this feeling like I need to get up or go check something right then. Like there’s a motor running under my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you have mood swings, where your mood changes quickly or unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get frustrated, sure, but I wouldn’t say my mood flips suddenly out of nowhere. At least… not that I’ve really noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. If I’m sitting with a client or in a chess meeting, my leg’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen. Sometimes I’ll just keep twisting a cable tie in my fingers until someone gives me that look.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these difficulties with focusing and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Pretty early, I guess. My mother still brings it up—grade school report cards saying I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Mostly in technical conversations. If I think I know the answer, it kind of jumps out before the other person’s done. I’ll talk over them, or sometimes reach for the keyboard too soon. I know it’s rude. I just… do it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like grabbing something or doing something physical without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… not usually in a physical way, I don’t think. I might be quick to do something before I’ve fully thought it through, but grabbing things or acting out like that… I haven’t really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry over small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get irritated like anyone, but I haven’t really tracked it as a problem. I don’t know that I’d say I get really angry over small things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the bigger problems.\n\nIf I don’t have something external keeping me on track—like the old ticketing system or an escalation calendar—my day just turns into… piles of sticky notes, half-finished emails, chess club reminders, parts orders. And then I can’t really tell what needs to happen first until something is already overdue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m not usually the loudest person in the room. Maybe if it’s something technical or something I’m interested in, I can go on a bit, but I haven’t really noticed it as a regular thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing physical things on impulse, like grabbing something or jumping up from your seat, without thinking about it first?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not so much grabbing things, but if I’m supposed to sit still and just listen, I get this feeling like I need to get up or go check something right then. Like there’s a motor running under my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you have mood swings, where your mood changes quickly or unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get frustrated, sure, but I wouldn’t say my mood flips suddenly out of nowhere. At least… not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty waiting for your turn in lines or when waiting for something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… waiting is pretty hard for me. Like if I’m on hold with a vendor or sitting in a clinic waiting room, I get really restless. I’ll keep checking the time, sighing, and wanting to move things along before it’s really my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense emotional reactions to things that wouldn't normally bother other people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really. I can get irritated, but “intense” reactions, like over things most people would shrug off—I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms like difficulty focusing, restlessness, and impulsivity significantly improve or worsen at any point in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it got a lot worse after I was fired. That was about… almost two years ago, maybe a year and ten months. I lost the routines and systems that kept me on track, and since then it’s caused real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for ADHD?\n\nPatient: No… not officially. I haven’t really been treated for it before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m having a hard time keeping my mind on things long enough to finish them. Even important stuff at work—like tracing a client’s network issue—I’ll look up and realize I’ve been rereading the same log entries over and over and still don’t really know what they said.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty often. If I’m sitting with a client or in a chess meeting, my leg’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen. Sometimes I’ll just keep twisting a cable tie in my fingers until someone gives me that look.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these difficulties with focusing and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Pretty early, I guess. My mother still brings it up—grade school report cards saying I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Mostly in technical conversations. If I think I know the answer, it kind of jumps out before the other person’s done. I’ll talk over them, or sometimes reach for the keyboard too soon. I know it’s rude. I just… do it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like grabbing something or doing something physical without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… not usually in a physical way, I don’t think. I might be quick to do something before I’ve fully thought it through, but grabbing things or acting out like that… I haven’t really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry over small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get irritated like anyone, but I haven’t really tracked it as a problem. I don’t know that I’d say I get really angry over small things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the bigger problems.\n\nIf I don’t have something external keeping me on track—like the old ticketing system or an escalation calendar—my day just turns into… piles of sticky notes, half-finished emails, chess club reminders, parts orders. I’m not usually the loudest person in the room. Maybe if it’s something technical or something I’m interested in, I can go on a bit, but I haven’t really noticed it as a regular thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing physical things on impulse, like grabbing something or jumping up from your seat, without thinking about it first?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not so much grabbing things, but if I’m supposed to sit still and just listen, I get this feeling like I need to get up or go check something right then. Like there’s a motor running under my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you have mood swings, where your mood changes quickly or unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get frustrated, sure, but I wouldn’t say my mood flips suddenly out of nowhere. If I’m sitting with a client or in a chess meeting, my leg’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen. Sometimes I’ll just keep twisting a cable tie in my fingers until someone gives me that look.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these difficulties with focusing and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Pretty early, I guess. My mother still brings it up—grade school report cards saying I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Mostly in technical conversations. If I think I know the answer, it kind of jumps out before the other person’s done. I’ll talk over them, or sometimes reach for the keyboard too soon. I know it’s rude. I just… do it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like grabbing something or doing something physical without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I might be quick to do something before I’ve fully thought it through, but grabbing things or acting out like that… I haven’t really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry over small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get irritated like anyone, but I haven’t really tracked it as a problem. I mean, I get irritated like anyone, but I haven’t really tracked it as a problem.
